ARTICLE
•
Serv. Soc. Soc. 147 (02)
•
2024
•
https://doi.org/10.1590/0101-6628.423
link
copy
The captain’s social policy: Bolsonarism, neo-Malthusianism, eugenics and militarization in Brazil
Authorship
SCIMAGO INSTITUTIONS RANKINGS
Abstract:
This text aims to present a reflection on the social policy of the government of Jair Messias Bolsonaro, president of Brazil from 2019 to 2022. It starts from an analysis of Bolsonaro and bolsonarism in the context of extreme neoliberalism and the management of the state based on neo-Malthusian, eugenic, and militarized principles. This reflection is the result of bibliographic and documentary research, as well as observations made as a teacher, lecturer, and researcher.
Keywords:
Social policy in Brazil; Bolsonarism and social policy; Bolsonaro and militarization; Eugenics and social policy; Far-right in Brazil

[SRC-6ebb8a72a54e0ab0 UNIT 6 | extracted HTML block 6]
Resumo:
Este texto objetiva apresentar uma reflexão sobre a política social do governo de Jair Messias Bolsonaro, presidente do Brasil entre 2019 e 2022. Para tanto, parte da análise sobre Bolsonaro e do bolsonarismo num contexto de neoliberalismo de extremos e gestão do Estado sob bases neomalthusianas, eugenistas e militarizadas. Esta reflexão é resultado de pesquisa bibliográfica, documental e de observações como docente, conferencista e pesquisadora.
Palavras-chave:
Política social no Brasil; Bolsonarismo e política social; Bolsonaro e militarização; Eugenia e política social; Extrema-direita
Abstract:
This text aims to present a reflection on the social policy of the government of Jair Messias Bolsonaro, president of Brazil from 2019 to 2022. It starts from an analysis of Bolsonaro and bolsonarism in the context of extreme neoliberalism and the management of the state based on neo-Malthusian, eugenic, and militarized principles. This reflection is the result of bibliographic and documentary research, as well as observations made as a teacher, lecturer, and researcher.
Keywords:
Social policy in Brazil; Bolsonarism and social policy; Bolsonaro and militarization; Eugenics and social policy; Far-right in Brazil
Introduction
Jair Messias Bolsonaro, president of Brazil from 2019 to 2022, is called “captain” by his supporters. More than an epithet, the rank reveals his military career, which began during the leaden years of the military regime, the height of censorship, violence and truculence of the Brazilian civil-military dictatorship. Jairo
Nicolau and Alceu Nunes (2020
) note that Bolsonaro’s hallmark is not his agenda of customs or public safety, but his explicit defense of the military regime. The captain Bolsonaro is known for his public repulsion to human rights and became one of the names of the contemporary far-right, as
Carvalho and Trench (2019
) and João
Rocha (2023
) point out.
But what was the Bolsonaro government’s social policy proposal? In classrooms, conferences and meetings with professionals from different areas and political spectrums, the answer has been unanimous: the Emergency Aid instituted during the Covid-19 Pandemic. This maxim was even corroborated by Bolsonaro in his campaign for re-election in 2022. However, like any ideology, this assumption is partial. It reflects the prism and interests of a dominant class and therefore hides aspects of a reality that is more complex than it appears to be.
Social policy is one of the modern State’s responses to pressure from the working class in the face of the contradictions inherent in capitalism. It takes the form of benefits, aid, programs and services offered to workers and their families, through public or private institutions, covering social rights such as health, welfare, social assistance, food, housing, among others. If social policy
appears
as such in everyday life, at the same time its role in the reproduction of the working class (and of capital itself) remains hidden. In the Bolsonaro government, however, it has been even more restricted.
De-financed and far removed from the logic of social rights, the captain’s social policy can only be understood as part of a project based on the barbarism of extreme neoliberalism which, in Brazil, has found a vector in Bolsonarism and its neo-Malthusian, eugenicist and militarized management of the State. These reflections are the result of bibliographical research dedicated to current literature on Bolsonaro, Bolsonarism and the far-right; documentary research, focused on institutional publications between 2018 and 2022, such as government plans, authorities’ speeches transcripts, reports, statistical data and others; and observations as a teacher, lecturer and researcher, in Brazil and abroad.
1
1. Barbarism as the only alternative in a game of rigged dice
- 190
2

[SRC-6ebb8a72a54e0ab0 UNIT 7 | extracted HTML block 7]
, what’s your emergency?
- Officer, it’s because there’s nothing for us to eat here at home and I’m hungry. My mother only has flour and cornmeal to eat (sic.)
- How old are you?
- I’m 11 years old.
- Who do you live with?
- I live with my mother only.
- Do you, your mother... and do you have any other siblings?
- Yes, I have... Five...
On August 2, 2022, the Military Police received this call from an 11-year-old child living in the metropolitan region of Belo Horizonte in Minas Gerais, Brazil (see
Hunger..., 2022
). A police car went to the scene. Perhaps it was a situation of neglect or mistreatment on the part of the mother. It had to be investigated. But what the police and the media found was a Black family made up of a single Black mother and her six children, who were very well cared for. Unemployed, surviving on R$250 reais ($50) from Brazil Aid, she didn’t even know about her son’s phone call. The neglect there, as is usually the case, was not the mother’s, but the State’s, and they really had nothing to eat. Shocked, the military set up donations and a campaign with the support of local businessmen and any civilian could collaborate by calling the 35th Police Battalion.
This is the summary of Brazil under the captain’s government: hunger, militarization of life, voluntarism, dismantling of social policies and the spectacularization of poverty, racism and sexism. Hunger is an emergency, but it shouldn’t be a 911 police case. Despite the significant attitude of the police officers, the question is: why did that child call the Military Police? Just the police, the State institution that is most present in the lives of poor and Black families. It’s likely that this child didn’t know about the rates of police lethality against young Black people in the country - so alarming that they’ve been called genocide. Why can’t his family rely on other institutions? To get to these answers, it’s essential to analyze how Bolsonaro has expressed a neoliberalism of extremes in Brazil.
Bolsonaro rose as a “myth” between the June 2013 Journeys and a set of anti-corruption investigations called Operation Car Wash (2014-2021), but Bolsonarism became bigger than the president-elect in 2018. Bolsonarism can be characterized as a political and ideological movement that, from the second decade of the 2000s, condensed a set of extremist positions in Brazil. Although it contains elements specific to Brazilian social formation, Bolsonarism has emerged in the current context of the internationalization of fascism.
Against a backdrop of a deepening capital crisis, flexible accumulation, the destruction of labor rights, permanent fiscal adjustment and the underfunding of social policies, the far right has risen up in different parts of the world with neoliberal, ultranationalist, populist, anti-immigration, pro-conservatism, science denialist agendas and a reaction to the achievements of historically discriminated groups. In Hungary, Viktor Orbán, since 2010; in Austria, Sebastian Kurz, chancellor from 2017 to 2021; in the United States, Donald Trump, elected in 2016 with the support of Steve Bannon; in Brazil, Jair Bolsonaro in 2019; in the United Kingdom, the Brexit decision in 2020; in Argentina, Javier Milei, president since 2023; and others.
Living through the hell of capitalism on Earth, the followers of the myth have elevated Jair to the status of Messiah. With the motto “God, country and family”, common in extremist movements, such as Italian fascism (1921) and Brazilian integralism (1932), Bolsonarism adopted as dogmas - therefore unquestionable and always justifiable - principles such as nationalism, authoritarianism; unrestricted freedom (individual and market); militarism; anti-communism; sexism; religious fundamentalism; pro-armamentism; denial of racism; conservatism; politics of hate; LGBTQIA+phobia; and, of course, the defense of a minimal State for social and labor rights, but ready to cater to the “god” market.
In addition to the above, Bolsonarism, whose guru was astrologer Olavo de Carvalho, is based on other identifiers, such as populism, persecution of what it calls “cultural Marxism”, its own aesthetics, scientific denialism, fake news, opposition to environmental protection, to native and traditional societies, and the idea that the country should be under the tutelage of the military. In Brazil, marked by a neoliberalism of extremes, of which Bolsonaro is both creature and (re)creator, reality is presented in binomials that always lead to the “choice” of barbarism as the only possible alternative, in a game of rigged dice.
At the “Women for Life and Family” meeting in Novo Hamburgo (RS), a southerly city in Brazil, in September 2022, Jair Bolsonaro asked the women the following question: “When you need to change a tire alone on the street and someone come towards you, would you rather have a Maria da Penha Law or a gun in your purse?” (
Sanches, 2022
). They immediately replied: “A pistol!” In fact, faced with this situation, what would you rather have: the gun or the law? This question naturalizes and assumes, firstly, the “inefficiency” of the State’s bureaucratic-administrative apparatus in guaranteeing what is provided for in the law protecting women; and, secondly, it advocates that in cases of imminent violence, there is only one possible response: more violence. Data from the Brazilian Public Security Forum (
Sanches, 2022
) reveals that every seven hours a woman is murdered in Brazil, the majority of whom are Black. Thus, the question and the answer naturalize sexism and racism.
Bolsonaro’s ideology creates and recreates unjust binomials, which distance perspectives and actions from the ills produced by capitalism. Its politics of fear produces tricky, binomial questions and answers: Do you prefer the Maria da Penha Law
3
or a gun? Call the police or starve to death? The Shooting Club or the Library (
Moratelli, 2002

[SRC-6ebb8a72a54e0ab0 UNIT 8 | extracted HTML block 8]
)? Printed ballots or fraud? The family or “gender ideology”? Precarious work or no income? Staying at home during the Pandemic or saving the economy (
“Economia..., 2020
).
And what do all these questions and answers tell us about the ethos of Bolsonarism? Only in the face of a dystopian reality, a state of nature along Hobbesian lines, without human and social rights, social and public policies, do such questions and their answers make sense. They operate by producing limited and limiting realities. And it was under this logic that the country faced the Covid-19 Pandemic.
2. “I’m the Messiah, but I don’t work miracles”:
4
Bolsonarism’s neo-Malthusian, eugenicist and militarized project of State management in Covid-19
While in the Catholic tradition the Messiah came to fulfill the prophecies of the Old Testament and redeem humanity from its sins, Jair Messias Bolsonaro, by action or inaction in the Covid-19 Pandemic, like an angel of death, fulfilled his own self-fulfilling prophecies and led hundreds of thousands of Brazilians to the “valley of the shadow of death” (Psalm 23:4), exposing a neo-Malthusian, eugenicist and militarized State management project.
Denounced for crimes against humanity (
Nicas, 2021
), the captain, for
Castilho and Lemos (2021
), used the State machine to deliberately practice necropolitics. In fact, despite its extensive public health system and high vaccination coverage, Brazil has lost almost 700,000 lives, which represents 10% of deaths from Covid-19 in the world, earning it the unfortunate title of the country with the second highest number of deaths.
5
From 2020 to 2022, Bolsonaro became internationally known for denying the seriousness of Covid-19, defending the false thesis of “herd immunity” (
Matoso; Gomes, 2021
), promoting ineffective drugs instead of vaccines, rejecting masks and social distancing protocols from the World Health Organization (WHO). Phrases such as “the economy cannot stop” were fired like bullets at workers who, with no alternative, exposed themselves to the virus in order to make a living. Bolsonaro went so far as to say that only the “weak, sick and elderly” (
Valfré, 2020
) should be worried and, in 2021, when the country reached 545,000 people killed by Covid-19, he declared: “my background as an athlete will protect me from the virus” (
Bolsonaro, 2021
). These statements do not express mere denialism or ignorance, but a militarized, neo-Malthusian and eugenicist State project.
This project prioritized ‘contamination,’ disregarding the disproportionate impact it would have on elderly individuals, people with disabilities, those with comorbidities, and those without the financial resources to maintain social isolation. At the same time, it exempted the State and society from protecting fundamental rights, including for these groups. In the “struggle for survival”, the right to life would be for the “fittest” and the virus would make this eugenicist selection (
Wegner; Souza; Carvalho, 2020
;
Costa, 2021

[SRC-6ebb8a72a54e0ab0 UNIT 9 | extracted HTML block 9]
;
Góes, 2023
).
In the second half of the 20th century, in a context of economic crisis, neo-Malthusianism recovered the theories of the pastor Thomas Malthus (1766-1834), who argued that exponential population growth would confront resources that would increase linearly, leading fatally to scarcity and poverty. For Malthus, periods of scarcity discouraged human reproduction, helping to “naturally” control the population. Malthus’ ideas somewhat influenced Charles Darwin (1809-1882), author of The Origin of Species (1859), in his concept of “natural selection”. According to this concept, in nature, the adaptive characteristics necessary for the survival and reproduction of an organism are developed and passed on to future generations, allowing it to evolve over time (
Bolsanello, 1996
;
Galvêas, 1996
).
Popularized in 19th century Western Europe, when inequalities typical of capitalism became evident, intellectuals such as Herbert Spencer (1820-1903) soon applied these ideas to society, creating the concept of “social Darwinism” to explain that, as in the natural world, life in society would be marked by the “struggle for survival”. Competition, scarcity and adaptation to these changes ensured that only the “fittest” would survive and pass on new characteristics to future generations (
Bolsanello, 1996
).
This influenced Francis Galton (1822-1911) in the creation of eugenics, which, regardless of its aspects (negative, positive, preventive), sought through science and politics to “perfect” humans by accelerating their “evolution” through the hereditary transmission of “desirable” characteristics. Eugenics, it must be said, uses racist and ableist notions to define these “desirable” and “undesirable” characteristics, and to classify groups as “fit” and “unfit”. “Survival” would be guaranteed only to the “best” of the species (
Stepan, 2005
;
Diwan, 2022
).
Eugenics, with its premise of “improvement”, manifested differently in various countries but universally targeted non-white individuals and those with disabilities, reflecting a shared racist ideology. In Nazi Germany, eugenics took on an extreme form, while in the United States, sterilization programs, as exemplified by the Buck v. Bell case, shocked the world. In Brazil and other Latin American countries during the early 20th century, eugenicists focused on the gradual “improvement” of the population through education, child-rearing practices, nutrition, sexual education, hygiene, and moral behavior. Although some instances of sterilization occurred, this was not the predominant form of eugenics in Latin America, as noted by
Stepan (2005
).
But in the Covid-19 Pandemic, neo-Malthusian theories, social Darwinism and eugenics have resurfaced, naturalizing deaths as necessary for “natural selection” and population balance. This reinforced ageism, racism, ableism and, of course, the supremacy of capital over labor in the neoliberalism of extremes - and condemned to death thousands read as “unfit” or “weak” and, in Bolsonaro’s words, “those without an athlete’s record” (
Coronavirus..., 2020
). In Brazil, this has justified and guided government actions. The denial of vaccination and masks was put forward as expression of “individual freedom”, and the “Covid kit” (with hydroxychloroquine, ivermectin and azithromycin) - cheaper than investing in intensive treatment (
Betim, 2021
) - became State policy, ignoring the principles of the Unified Health System (in Portuguese: SUS).
In 2021, the Parliamentary Commission of Inquiry (in Portuguese: CPI) into the Pandemic received a complaint that the federal government and a health operator focused on the elderly, Prevent Senior, signed an agreement to test the Covid kit between March and April 2020. The result was catastrophic, but the research with the results was allegedly manipulated to prove the success of this “early treatment”. In addition, professionals were instructed not to wear masks to “spread” the virus, accelerating “herd immunity” (

[SRC-6ebb8a72a54e0ab0 UNIT 10 | extracted HTML block 10]
Balza, 2021
). The supposed “experiment” would have made Josef Mengele proud.
Weber Góes (2023
) points to these and other examples that support the eugenics thesis during the Pandemic, such as the high rates of contamination among incarcerated people and the way the virus affected Brazilian workers. Research by Fiocruz (
Pereira et al., 2022
) shows that between 2020 and 2021, Black people in Brazil died most from Covid-19 and received the fewest vaccinations; indigenous people also suffered disproportionately from the effects of the health crisis.
Racism against the Black population, expressed in restricted socio-economic conditions, precarious housing, lower wages, prevalence of comorbidities (diabetes, hypertension), exposure to food and nutritional insecurity, helps to understand their high mortality rates during Covid-19. It is not genetic determinants that ascribe “inferiority” to some groups, but socially constituted and institutionally reproduced hierarchies that prevent their access to dignified living conditions.
To “solve” the Covid-19 crisis, the Bolsonaro government has not resorted to social policies or the defense of human and social rights. In addition to neo-Malthusian and eugenicist foundations, it has resorted to what is also typical of his government: the militarized management of the State.
In almost three decades of political life, Bolsonaro has not abandoned his military background. In 2011, he opposed the creation of the National Truth Commission, which investigated the crimes of the dictatorship, and this position, according to João
Rocha (2021
), consolidated the support of part of the barracks for the captain. In 2016, during the vote on the impeachment of President Dilma Rousseff, Bolsonaro paid homage to Col. Brilhante Ustra - the torturer of Rousseff and hundreds of others during the civil-military dictatorship (
Barba; Wentzel, 2016
), pointing out that the left lost in 1964 and would lose again in 2016. His vote was for freedom and against communism, reaffirming this ideological position (Speech..., 2016).
Democratic play and institutional politics didn’t discipline the captain’s despotic militarism, but vice versa. It was Bolsonaro who wanted to discipline the world of politics and social life on his own terms. In a dubious paradox, in 2019 the force that once undermined democracy returned to power, this time through democracy itself.
6
In captain Bolsonaro’s government, in addition to himself and his vice president, General Mourão, several high-ranking positions have been filled by military reservists. Many of them were active between 1964 and 1985, during the civil-military dictatorship. With the argument of technical competence and uprightness of character, supposedly typical of militarism, almost half of his ministers were military. At other levels, there was a 70% increase in the number of military personnel occupying civilian positions, such as in communications, social security (Instituto Nacional do Seguro Social - INSS), and health and education policies (
Lynch; Cassimiro, 2022
;
Schmidt, 2022
).
At the height of the Pandemic, Bolsonaro chose a military expert in logistics, General Pazuello (2020-2021), as minister of health. The minister defined his relationship with the president: “one gives the orders and the other obeys” (
Mazui, 2020
). The unquestioning obeying of orders can be part of the military environment, and it seems that this authoritarian premise has spread, trampling on the concept of health, assistance, social security, food, housing, education and work, under fair conditions, as constitutional rights.
3. “Brazil above anything, God above all”
7

[SRC-6ebb8a72a54e0ab0 UNIT 11 | extracted HTML block 11]
: Bolsonaro’s social policy
Market economies have historically been the greatest instrument for
generating income, employment, prosperity and social inclusion.
Thanks to Liberalism, billions of people are being saved from poverty
all over the world
(
Phoenix Project, 2018
, n. p.).
The motto and epigraph of this section are from a 2018 document by then-presidential candidate Jair Bolsonaro called “Bolsonarismo’s path to prosperity: a proposed government plan”, or “Fenix Project (Projeto Fênix)”. The plan includes guidelines and objectives for the economy, governance, infrastructure, security, science, international relations, health, welfare, social assistance, education, etc. The thread that drives his proposals is economic liberalism, identified as the solution to inequalities, violence, unemployment and poverty. As a result, the emphasis is on individual freedom, market freedom, public security, the arming of civilians, and the reduction of the size of the State. There is no mention of “social rights” and “social policy” in the plan, and “human rights” are focused primarily on the victims of violence.
8
In the area of health, the Phoenix Project foresaw the State Doctor to look after the population living in hard-to-reach places and “needy” people; training to transform Community Health Agents into preventive health technicians to act in the “control” of diseases; computerization of health units and implementation of the Interconnected National Electronic Health Record; universal endorsement of doctors in a public-private partnership; outdoor gyms through the “Family Health Program” - a term that has not been used since 2006 - providing for the insertion of professionals who were already part of the Family Health Strategy teams.
In the government plan proposed by Bolsonaro, social security was considered within the context of economic policy. The proposal put forth was to replace the current pay-as-you-go model, in which active workers finance the benefits of retirees, pensioners, and other beneficiaries, with a capitalization model. As
Granemann (2020
) observed, the capitalization model, which is based on the logic of private insurance, differs from social security as a collective right and part of social security. Capitalization is individual, with the responsibility for contributions resting with the worker, who deposits amounts in an account that is subject to investments and market fluctuations. To encourage membership, the Phoenix Project proposed the creation of a fund.
In the Phoenix Project, the Unified Social Assistance System (SUAS) was not taken into consideration. The Bolsa Família Program (PBF) was referenced on two occasions: first, in the proposal that each worker receive a minimum income equal to or greater than the value of the benefit; and second, in the argument that PBF and minimum income were creations inspired by liberals like Milton Friedman. Indeed, as Silva (2019) and
Amaral (2022
) elucidate, Friedman, an economist from the Chicago School, advocated for a negative income tax, namely, an addition to the income of those earning below a specified threshold. However, this concept diverges from the notion of minimum income espoused by the left, as liberals contend that direct income transfer supplants the necessity for other State interventions through social and public policies.
The Phoenix Project provides insight into the actions of the Bolsonaro government with regard to social policy, demonstrating that these actions are not the result of chance, but rather part of a deliberate plan to shape the future of the country. The solution to the public debt is privatization and a reduction in spending, with the generation of a surplus being a priority. He reaffirmed the tenets of Michel Temer’s labor counter-reform (Law No. 13,467/2017), which has resulted in the deterioration of workers’ rights. With respect to education, he advocated for the prioritization of secondary and technical education, as well as the “purge of Paulo Freire’s ideology”, with an emphasis on mathematics, Portuguese, and science. In higher education, he proposed the incorporation of entrepreneurship and public-private partnerships into the curriculum.
In their 2020 analysis of the federal government’s budget documents (PPA, LDO 2020 and LOA 2020),
Behring, Fiuza and Souza (2020
) observed trends in the financing of social policies and identified the “institutionalization of social inequalities”.
Raichelis and Arregui (2021
) posit that the social pact enshrined in the 1988 Constitution unravelled amidst the confluence of economic, political and health crises. The fiscal constraints imposed by the crisis rendered social rights incompatible with the budget, necessitating an appeal to forms of solidarity and entrepreneurship. This perspective aligns with the stance of those who advocate for a restrictive concept of minimum income (
Behring, 2009
).

[SRC-6ebb8a72a54e0ab0 UNIT 12 | extracted HTML block 12]
In essence, the administration of Jair Bolsonaro has reintroduced the concepts of first-ladyism, solidarity, and voluntarism as core tenets of Christian and patriotic ideology. Jair, who identifies as both “conservative” and Catholic, was baptized in the Jordan River in 2016. This led evangelicals to view him as a “faithful brother” and to vote for him, in accordance with the tenets of “Dominion Theology” (
Alexandre, 2020
;
Rocha, 2021
). Evangelical Michele Bolsonaro played a pivotal role in the National Program to Encourage Volunteering, which was formally known as Pátria Voluntária (Voluntary Homeland) (2019). While promoting volunteering and espousing Christian values, the government simultaneously undermined popular involvement in the monitoring and oversight of public and social policies. Conferences were vacated and social control bodies were abolished, including the National Council for Food and Nutritional Security (CONSEA). In 2022, Brazil was once again identified as a country facing significant challenges related to food insecurity. The prevalence of hunger was evidenced by the queues for bone donations, the malnutrition of indigenous peoples (cf.
Fila, 2021
;
Granchi, 2023
), and the link to the child in Belo Horizonte.
As foreseen in the 2018 document of intent, the government’s focus was on income transfers. In 2019, it offered a thirteenth to Programa Bolsa Família (PBF) beneficiaries;
9
in 2021, during the Pandemic, it replaced the PBF with Brazil Aid;
10
and then created the “Brazilian Gas Aid”
11
. Suas, in turn, suffered a 40% budget cut between 2019 and 2020, as
Castilho and Lemos (2021
) point out.
12
Concurrently, the Secret Budget
13
was introduced, effectively superseding the public and transparent planning of public and social policies. The budgetary reduction had a significant impact on the Social Assistance Reference Center (CRAS) units, which played a pivotal role in providing social assistance to the population during the Pandemic.
One might inquire as to the status of emergency aid. The aid was a cash transfer benefit for workers (both informal and unemployed) during the period of the Covid-19 Pandemic, established through a set of laws, provisional measures, and decrees between April 2020 and March 2022. The benefit was distributed in installments, with amounts varying from R$250 to R$1,200, contingent upon the family profile in question. In 2020, over 68 million individuals received the benefit, with the majority being women (
Dataprev, 2020
). However, the Emergency Aid was not a social policy of the Bolsonaro government per se; rather, it was a popular achievement that occurred despite the government’s involvement.

[SRC-6ebb8a72a54e0ab0 UNIT 13 | extracted HTML block 13]
The proposal to establish an emergency assistance program emerged from the consolidation of several legislative initiatives from a range of political parties, spanning the center to the left. Many of these individuals lacked a direct affiliation with the government. The captain and his team were, in fact, opposed to the proposed aid. When they eventually accepted the idea, they proposed an amount of R$200, which was to be distributed only to the self-employed and those who were “unassisted” (
Martello; Rodrigues, 2020
). Nevertheless, Bolsonaro benefited from the popularity generated by the program. This provides context for his Stated intention to reinstate the benefit in 2021 and to “turbocharge” Auxílio Brasil in 2022, an election year, by expanding the number of beneficiaries and the amounts payable.
14
This emphasis on direct income transfer also reveals Friedman’s vision of “minimum income” as a substitute for a system of free public services (
Barrocal, 2020
). Paulo Guedes, who studied where Milton Friedman was a professor, even proposed distributing vouchers for the impoverished population to access private health and education services: “
We’re going to have to do the same thing with health as we did with emergency aid. Are poor people sick? Give them a voucher. Want to go to [Albert] Einstein Hospita
15
l? Go to Einstein. He wants to go to SUS, he can use his voucher wherever he wants. There’s no management in public health
” (cf.
Preite Sobrinho, 2021
).
In Bolsonaro’s new language, there is no public, free and universal social policy, as stated in the 1988 Federal Constitution, integrating health as part of Brazilian Social Security. Under this government, the Popular Pharmacy, which provides free or low-cost medicines, has been de-funded. The Therapeutic Communities were transferred from Health to the Ministry of Citizenship and there was a 78% increase in the number of entities, most of them religious, contracted to run these institutions (see
Machado, 2022
).
SUS has resisted through the commitment of its workers, municipal and state managers, in the face of increased maternal mortality and reduced infant vaccination coverage (
Castilho; Lemos, 2021
), in addition to the lack of guidelines from the Ministry of Health. In Social Security, Bolsonaro carried out a “reform” - aligned with extreme neoliberal austerity and a sexist vision, among others - which disregarded women’s working conditions and increased their retirement age. In terms of labor relations, he proposed the Green and Yellow Card, with flexible hiring of young people at a low cost to the employer.
If one of Bolsonaro’s mottos is “God, country and family”, the actions of the captain’s government have followed the neoliberal Trinity: privatization, outsourcing and financialization. Faced with this, who else could that 11-year-old call?
4. “You will know the truth and the truth will set you free”
16
Elio Gaspari (2014
;
2016

[SRC-6ebb8a72a54e0ab0 UNIT 14 | extracted HTML block 14]
) posits that during the 1964 Dictatorship in Brazil, the military was comprised of disparate factions: developmentalists (advocates of modernization and economic growth), hardliners (those who favored repression), moderates (proponents of gradual political and social reform), and technocrats (admirers of public administration). Despite potential discrepancies, they were proponents of the regime, which employed both assistance and violence to maintain its hold on power, as
Behring and Boschetti (2017
) illustrate. To underscore the significance of social policy for the regime’s legitimization, the authors utilize the concept of the “assistance-industrial-technocratic-military complex,” as postulated by Vicente Faleiros (Ibid.).
Following this premise, it is possible to argue that, close to hard-line, Jair Bolsonaro’s government, with its authoritarian face, sought consensus not necessarily through social policy, but through rhetoric and actions centered on extreme neoliberalism. To do this, it used neo-Malthusian, eugenicist, Christian fundamentalist and militarized bases for state management. Thus, social policy was seen as ancillary and dispensable, its defenders as “communists” and, therefore, targets to be neutralized.
Extreme neoliberalism rejects labor, social and even civil rights for certain groups, promoting the maxim that welfare, assistance, health, education, housing, food, etc., rather than rights, should be individual, accessed only through the private sector. Aligned with the far right, this nefarious ideology is spreading throughout society and the State, steeped in intolerance, the imposition of “traditional values”, racism, sexism and religious fundamentalism, which attack groups, naturalize inequalities and promise prosperity, while at the same time eroding democracy.
Its effects did not end with Bolsonaro’s defeat in 2022. The success of Pablo Marçal’s candidacy demonstrates that this scenario of chaos, amplified by social networks, allows for the emergence of new “phenomena”, which although aligned with far-right are independent of Bolsonarism. On this list of examples is federal deputy pastor Sóstenes Cavalcante, author of the Bill (1.904/2024) to criminalize victims, including children, for terminating pregnancies in the event of rape (
Becker, 2024
). In 2024, even with Lula, a progressive president in power, the ideas of extreme neoliberalism in Brazil are still alive, generating binary and restrictive questions and answers:
would you rather
be imprisoned or give birth to a child as a result of rape? Do you prefer Bolsonaro or Pablo Marçal? The collective challenge in Brazil and around the world is to regain the capacity to dream and act beyond binomials.
